gates (under inclusion) such that:

(i)

S
contains the true input gates, i.e., it contains
\{g\in C_{\mathsf{inp}}\mid\nu(g)=1\}
;

(ii)

for any
g
such that
\mu(g)=\lor
, if some input gate of
g
is
in
S
, then
g
is in
S
;

(iii)

for any
g
such that
\mu(g)=\land
, if all input gates of
g
are
in
S
, then
g
is in
S
.

Proof.

The operator used in Algorithm
1
is clearly
monotone, so by the Knaster–Tarski theorem, the outcome of the
computation is the intersection of all set of gates satisfying the
conditions in Proposition
26
.
∎

Algorithm
1
is a naive fixpoint algorithm running in quadratic time, but we show that the same output can be computed in linear time with Algorithm
2
.

Proposition

(
27
)
.

Given any monotone cycluit
C
and Boolean valuation
\nu\!
of
C
, we can compute the
evaluation
\nu^{\prime}\!
of
C
in linear time.

Proof.

We use Algorithm
2
.
We first prove the claim about the running time. The preprocessing to compute

M
is linear-time in
C
(we enumerate at most once every wire), and the rest of the algorithm is clearly
in linear time as it is a variant of a DFS traversal of the graph, with the added
refinement that we only visit nodes that evaluate to
1
(i.e., OR-gates
with some input that evaluates to
1
, and AND-gates where all inputs
evaluate to
1
).

We now prove correctness. We use the characterization of
Proposition
26
. We first check that
S
satisfies the
properties:

(i)

S
contains the true input gates by construction.

(ii)

Whenever an OR-gate
g^{\prime}
has an input gate
g
in
S
, then, when
we added
g
to
S
, we have necessarily followed the wire
g\rightarrow g^{\prime}
and added
g^{\prime}
to
Q
, and later added it to
S
.

(iii)

Whenever an AND-gate
g^{\prime}
has
all its input gates
g^{\prime}
in
S
, there are two cases. The first case in when

g
has no input gates at all, in which case
S
contains it by
construction. The second case is where such input gates exist: in this
case, observe that
M[g^{\prime}]
was initially equal to the degree of
g^{\prime}
, and
that we decrement it for each input gate
g
of
g^{\prime}
that we add to
S
.
Hence, considering the last input gate
g
of
g^{\prime}
that we add to
S
, it
must be the case that
M[g^{\prime}]
reaches zero when we decrement it,
and then we add
g
to
Q
, and later to
S
.

Second, we check that
S
is minimal. Assume by contradiction that it
is not the case, and consider the first gate
g
which is
added to
S
while not being in the minimal Boolean valuation
S^{\prime}
. It cannot
be the case that
g
was added when initializing
S
, as we initialize
S
to
contain true input gates and AND-gates with no inputs, which must be true
also in
S^{\prime}
by the characterization of Proposition
26
. Hence,
we added
g
to
S
in a later step of the algorithm. However,
we notice that we must added
g
to
S
because of the value of its input
gates. By minimality of
g
, these input gates have the same value in
S
and
in
S^{\prime}
. This yields a contradiction, because the gates that we add to
S
are
added following the characterization of Proposition
26
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Input:

Monotone cycluit
C=(G,W,g_{0},\mu)
, Boolean valuation
\nu:C_{\mathsf{inp}}\to\{0,1\}

Output:

\{g\in C\mid\nu(g)=1\}

/*
Precompute the in-degree of
\land
gates */

for

g\in C
s.t.
\mu(g)=\land

do

1

M[g]\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left|\{g^{\prime}\in C\mid g^{\prime}\rightarrow g\}\right|

2

Q\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{g\in C_{\mathsf{inp}}\mid\nu(g)=1\}\cup\{g\in C\mid(\mu(C)=\land)\land M[g]=0\}

/*
as a stack */

S\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\emptyset

/*
as a bit array */

while

Q\neq\emptyset

do

3

pop
g
from
Q

4

if

g\notin S

then

5

add
g
to
S

6

for

g^{\prime}\in C\mid g\to g^{\prime}

do

7

if

\mu(g^{\prime})=\lor

then

8

push
g^{\prime}
into
Q

9

if

\mu(g^{\prime})=\land

then

10

M[g^{\prime}]\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}M[g^{\prime}]-1

11

if

M[g^{\prime}]=0

then

12

push
g^{\prime}
into
Q

13

14

return

S

Algorithm 2

Linear-time evaluation of monotone cycluits

.5
Stratified cycluits

We show the claim that a Boolean cycluit is stratified iff it contains no cycle of gates involving a
\neg
-gate, and that
a stratification function can be computed in linear time.

Proposition 48
.

Any Boolean cycluit
C
is stratified iff it it contains no cycle of gates involving a
\neg
-gate.
Moreover, a stratification function can be computed in linear time from
C
.

Proof.

To see why a stratified Boolean cycluit
C
cannot contain a cycle of gates involving a
\neg
-gate,
assume by contradiction that it has such a cycle
g_{1}\rightarrow g_{2}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow g_{n}\rightarrow g_{1}
. As
C
is stratified, there exists a stratification function
\zeta
.
From the properties of a stratification function, we know that
\zeta(g_{1})\leqslant\zeta(g_{2})\leqslant\cdots\leqslant\zeta(g_{1})
, so that we must have
\zeta(g_{1})=\cdots=\zeta(g_{n})
.
However, letting
g_{i}
be such that
\mu(g_{i})=\neg
, we know that

\zeta(g_{i-1})<\zeta(g_{i})
(or, if
i=1
,
\zeta(g_{n})<\zeta(g_{1})
),
so we have a contradiction.

We now prove the converse direction of the claim, i.e., that any
Boolean cycluit which does not contain a cycle of gates involving a
\neg
-gate must have a stratification function,
and show how to compute such a function in linear time.
Compute in linear time the strongly connected components (SCCs) of
C
, and a
topological sort of the SCCs. As the input gates of
C
do not themselves have
inputs, each of them must have their own SCC, and each such SCC must be a
leaf, so we can modify the topological sort by merging these SCCs
corresponding to input gates, and putting them first in the topological sort.
We define the function

\zeta
to map each gate
of
C
to the index number of its SCC in the topological sort, which ensures
in particular that the input gates of
C
are exactly the gates assigned
to
0
by
\zeta
.
This can be performed in linear time. Let us show that the result
\zeta
is
a stratification function:

•

For any edge
g\rightarrow g^{\prime}
, we have
\zeta(g)\leqslant\zeta(g^{\prime})
.
Indeed, either
g
and
g^{\prime}
are in the same strongly connected component and we
have
\zeta(g)=\zeta(g^{\prime})
, or they are not and in this case the edge

g\rightarrow g^{\prime}
witnesses that the SCC of
g
precedes that of
g^{\prime}
,
whence, by definition of a topological sort, it follows that
\zeta(g)<\zeta(g^{\prime})
.

•

For any edge
g\rightarrow g^{\prime}
where
\mu(g^{\prime})=\lnot
, we have
\zeta(g)<\zeta(g^{\prime})
. Indeed, by adapting the reasoning of the previous bullet
point, it suffices to show that
g
and
g^{\prime}
cannot be in the same
SCC. Indeed, assuming by contradiction that they are, by
definition of a SCC, there must be a path from
g^{\prime}
to
g
, and combining
this with the edge
g\rightarrow g^{\prime}
yields a cycle involving a

\lnot
-gate,
contradicting our assumption on
C
.∎

Proposition

(
30
)
.

We can compute
\nu(C)
in linear time in the stratified cycluit
C
and in
\nu
.

Proof.

Compute in linear time a stratification function
\zeta
of
C
using
Proposition
48
, and compute the evaluation following Definition
29
.
This can be performed in linear time.

To see why this evaluation is independent from the choice of
stratification, observe that any stratification function must clearly assign the
same value to all gates in an SCC. Hence, the
choosing a stratification function amounts to choosing
the stratum that we assign to each SCC. Further,
when an SCC
S
precedes another SCC
S^{\prime}
, the stratum of
S
must be no
higher than the stratum of
S^{\prime}
. So in fact the only freedom that we
have is to choose a topological sort of the SCCs, and optionally to
assign the same stratum to consecutive SCCs in the topological sort:
this amounts to “merging” some SCCs, and is only possible when there
are no
\lnot
-gates between them. Now, in the evaluation, it is clear
that the order in which we evaluate the SCCs makes no difference, nor
does it matter if some SCCs are evaluated simultaneously. Hence, the
evaluation of a stratified cycluit is well-defined.
∎

.6
Building provenance cycluits

Theorem

(
32
)
.

For any fixed alphabet
\Gamma
, given a
\overline{\Gamma}
-SATWA
A
and a

\Gamma
-tree
\mathcal{T}
, we can build
a stratified cycluit capturing the provenance of
A

on
\mathcal{T}

in time
O(\left|A\right|\cdot\left|\mathcal{T}\right|)
.
Moreover, this stratified cycluit has treewidth
O(\left|A\right|)
.

To prove Theorem
32
, we construct a cycluit
C^{A}_{T}
as follows, generalizing the
construction
of
[
ABS15b
]
.
For each node
w
of
T
, we create an input node
g_{w}^{\mathrm{i}}
, a
\lnot
-gate

g_{w}^{\lnot\mathrm{i}}
defined as
\mathrm{NOT}(g_{w}^{\mathrm{i}})
, and an OR-gate
g_{w}^{q}
for each state
q\in Q
. Now for each
g_{w}^{q}
, for
b\in\{0,1\}
, we consider the propositional
formula
\Delta(q,(\lambda(w),b))
, and we express it as a circuit that
captures this formula:
we let

g_{w}^{q,b}
be the output gate of that circuit, we
replace each variable
q^{\prime}
occurring positively by an
OR-gate
\bigvee_{w^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Nbh}(w)}g_{w^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}}
,
and we replace each variable
q^{\prime}
occurring negatively by the gate
g_{w}^{q^{\prime}}
.
We then define
g^{q}_{w}
as

\mathrm{OR}(\and(g_{w}^{\mathrm{i}},g_{w}^{q,0}),\and(g_{w}^{\lnot\mathrm{i}},g_{w}^{q,1}))
. Finally, we let the output gate of
C
be
g_{r}^{q_{\mathrm{I}}}
, where
r
is the root of
T
.

It is clear that this process runs in linear time in
\left|A\right|\cdot\left|T\right|
.
Moreover, for every node
w
of
T
, we create
O(\left|A\right|)
gates, and those gates
can only be connected between them or between gates created for the neighbors
of
w
. Hence, the treewidth of
C^{A}_{T}
is
O(\left|A\right|)
: we can compute
a tree decomposition of
C^{A}_{T}
where the underlying tree is
T
and where
each bag
b
corresponding to a node
w
of
T
contains the gates defined for
the node
w
and for the neighbors of
w
.
The proof of Theorem
32
then follows from the following claim:

Lemma 49
.

The cycluit
C^{A}_{T}
is a stratified cycluit capturing the provenance of
A

on
\langle T,\lambda\rangle
.

Proof.

We first show that
C\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}C^{A}_{T}
is a stratified cycluit.
Let
\zeta
be the stratification function
of the
\overline{\Gamma}
-SATWA
A
and let
\{0,\ldots,m\}
be its range.
We use
\zeta
to define
\zeta^{\prime}
as the following function from the gates
of
C
to
\{0,\ldots,m+1\}
:

•

For any input gate
g_{w}^{\mathrm{i}}
, we set

\zeta^{\prime}(g_{w}^{\mathrm{i}})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}0
and

\zeta^{\prime}(g_{w}^{\lnot\mathrm{i}})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}1
.

•

For an OR gate
g\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigvee_{w^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Nbh}(w)}g_{w^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}}
, we set

\zeta^{\prime}(g)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\zeta(q^{\prime})
.

•

For any state
g_{w}^{q}
, we set
\zeta^{\prime}(g_{w}^{n})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\zeta(q)+1
, and do the same for the intermediate AND-gates used in its
definition, as well as the gates in the two circuits that capture the
transitions
\Delta(q,(\lambda(w),b))
for
b\in\{0,1\}
, except
for the input gates of that circuit (i.e., gates of the form
\bigvee_{w^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Nbh}(w)}g_{w^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}}
, which are covered by the previous point, or

g^{q^{\prime}}_{w}
, which are covered by another application of that point).

Let us show that
\zeta^{\prime}
is indeed a stratification function for
C
. We first
observe that it is the case that the gates in stratum zero are precisely the
input gates. We then check the condition for the various possible wires:

•

g^{\mathrm{i}}_{w}\rightarrow g^{\lnot\mathrm{i}}_{w}
: by construction, we have

\zeta(g^{\mathrm{i}}_{w})<\zeta^{\prime}(g^{\lnot\mathrm{i}}_{w})
.

•

g\rightarrow g^{\prime}
where
g^{\prime}
is a gate of the form
g_{w}^{q}
and
g
is an
intermediate AND-gate in the definition of a
g_{w}^{q}
: by construction we
have
\zeta^{\prime}(g)=\zeta^{\prime}(g^{\prime})
, so in particular
\zeta^{\prime}(g)\leqslant\zeta^{\prime}(g^{\prime})
.

•

g\rightarrow g^{\prime}
where
g^{\prime}
is an intermediate AND-gate in the
definition of a gate of the form
g_{w}^{q}
, and
g
is
g^{\mathrm{i}}_{w}
or

g^{\lnot\mathrm{i}}_{w}
: by construction we have
\zeta^{\prime}(g)\in\{0,1\}
and

\zeta^{\prime}(g^{\prime})\geqslant 1
, so
\zeta^{\prime}(g)\leqslant\zeta^{\prime}(g^{\prime})
.

•

g\rightarrow g^{\prime}
where
g
is a gate in a circuit capturing
the propositional formula of some transition of
\Delta(q,\cdot)
without
being an input gate or a NOT-gate of this circuit, and
g^{\prime}
is also such a
gate, or is an intermediate AND-gate in the definition of
g_{w}^{q}
: then

g^{\prime}
cannot be a NOT-gate (remembering
that the propositional formulae of transitions only have negations on
literals), and
by construction we have
\zeta^{\prime}(g)=\zeta^{\prime}(g^{\prime})
.

•

g\rightarrow g^{\prime}
where
g
is of the
form
\bigvee_{w^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Nbh}(w)}g_{w^{\prime}}^{q}
, and
g^{\prime}
is
a gate in a circuit describing
\Delta(q^{\prime},\cdot)
or an intermediate
gate in the definition of
g^{q^{\prime}}_{w}
.
Then we have
\zeta^{\prime}(g)=\zeta(q)
and
\zeta^{\prime}(g^{\prime})=\zeta(q^{\prime})
,
and as
q
occurs as a positive literal in a transition of
q^{\prime}
, by
definition of
\zeta
being a transition function, we have
\zeta(q)\leqslant\zeta(q^{\prime})
. Now we have
\zeta^{\prime}(g)=\zeta(q)
and
\zeta^{\prime}(g^{\prime})=\zeta^{\prime}(q^{\prime})
by definition of
\zeta^{\prime}
, so we deduce that

\zeta^{\prime}(g)\leqslant\zeta^{\prime}(g^{\prime})
.

•

g\rightarrow g^{\prime}
where
g^{\prime}
is of the
form
\bigvee_{w^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Nbh}(w)}g_{w^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}}
, and
g
is
one of the
g_{w^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}}
. Then by definition of
\zeta^{\prime}
we have

\zeta^{\prime}(g)=\zeta(q^{\prime})
and
\zeta^{\prime}(g^{\prime})=\zeta(q^{\prime})
, so in
particular
\zeta^{\prime}(g)\leqslant\zeta^{\prime}(g^{\prime})
.

•

g\rightarrow g^{\prime}
where
g
is a NOT-gate in a circuit
capturing a propositional formula
\Delta(q^{\prime},(\lambda(w),b))
, and
g
is then necessarily a gate of the form

g^{q}_{w}
: then clearly
q^{\prime}
was
negated in
\varphi
so we had
\zeta(q)<\zeta(q^{\prime})
, and as by
construction we have
\zeta^{\prime}(g)=\zeta(q)
and
\zeta^{\prime}(g^{\prime})=\zeta(q^{\prime})
,
we deduce that
\zeta^{\prime}(g)<\zeta^{\prime}(g^{\prime})
.

We now show that
C
indeed captures the provenance of
A
on
\langle T,\lambda\rangle
.
Let
\nu:T\to\{0,1\}
be a Boolean valuation of the inputs of
C
, that we
extend to an evaluation
\nu^{\prime}:C\to\{0,1\}
of
C
.
We claim the following
equivalence
: for all
q
and
w
, there exists a run
\rho
of
A
on
\nu(T)

starting at
w
in state
q
if and only if
\nu(g_{w}^{q})=1
.

We prove this claim by induction on the stratum
\zeta(q)
of
q
. Up to adding
an empty first stratum, we can make sure that the base case is vacuous.
For the induction step, we prove each implication separately.

Forward direction.

First, suppose that there exists a run
\rho
starting at
w
in state
q
, and
let us show that
\nu^{\prime}(g^{q}_{w})=1
. We show by induction on the run (from
bottom to top) that for each node
y
of the run labeled by a
positive
state

(q^{\prime},w^{\prime})
we have
\nu^{\prime}(g^{q^{\prime}}_{w^{\prime}})=1
, and for every node
y
of the run
labeled by a
negative
state
\lnot(q^{\prime},w^{\prime})
we have
\nu^{\prime}(g^{q^{\prime}}_{w^{\prime}})=0
. The base case concerns the leaves, where there are three possible
subcases:

•

We may have
\lambda_{r}(y)=(q^{\prime},w^{\prime})
with
\zeta(q^{\prime})=i
, so
that
\Delta(q^{\prime},(\lambda(w^{\prime}),\nu(w^{\prime})))
is tautological. In
this case,
g_{w^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}}
is defined as

\mathrm{OR}(\and(g_{w^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{i}},g_{w^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime},1}),\and(g_{w^{\prime}}^{\lnot\mathrm{i}},g_{w^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime},0}))
. Hence, we know that
\nu(g_{w^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime},\nu(w)})=1
because the circuit is also tautological, and depending on whether

\nu(w)
is
0
or
1

we know that
\nu(g_{w^{\prime}}^{\lnot\mathrm{i}})=1
or
\nu(g_{w^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{i}})=1
, so this
proves the claim.

•

We may have
\lambda_{r}(y)=(q^{\prime},{w^{\prime}})
with
\zeta(q^{\prime})=j
for
j<i
.
By definition of the run
\rho
, this implies that there exists
a run starting at
{w^{\prime}}
in state
q^{\prime}
.
But then, by the induction on the strata (using the forward
direction of the
equivalence
), we must have
\nu(g_{w^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}})=1
.

•

We may have
\lambda_{r}(y)=\lnot(q^{\prime},{w^{\prime}})
with
\zeta(q^{\prime})=j
for
j<i
.
Then by definition there exists no run starting at
{w^{\prime}}
in state
q^{\prime}
.
Hence again by induction on the strata (using the backward
direction of the
equivalence
), we have that
\nu(g_{w^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}})=0
.

For the induction case on the run, where
y
is an internal node,
by definition of a run there is a subset
S=\{q_{P_{1}},\cdots,q_{P_{n}}\}
of
positive literals and a subset
N=\{\lnot q_{N_{1}},\cdots,\lnot q_{N_{m}}\}

of negative literals that satisfy
\varphi_{\nu(w^{\prime})}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Delta(q^{\prime},(\lambda(w^{\prime}),\nu(w^{\prime})))
such
that:

•

For all
q_{P_{k}}\in P
, there exists a child
y_{k}
of
y
with
\lambda_{r}(y_{k})=(q_{P_{k}},{w^{\prime}}_{k})

where
{w^{\prime}}_{k}\in\mathrm{Nbh}({w^{\prime}})
;

•

For all
\lnot q_{N_{k}}\in N
there is a child
y_{{w^{\prime}}_{k}}

of
y
with

\lambda_{r}(y_{w^{\prime}})=\lnot(q_{N_{k}},{w^{\prime}})
.

Then, by induction on the run, we
know that for all
q_{P_{k}}
we have
\nu(g_{{w^{\prime}}_{k}}^{q_{P_{k}}})=1
and for all

\lnot q_{N_{k}}
we have

\nu(g_{{w^{\prime}}}^{q_{N_{k}}})=0
. By construction of
C
, we have

\nu(g_{w^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}})=1
.
There are two cases:
either
\nu(w^{\prime})=1
or
\nu(w^{\prime})=0
. In the
first case, remember that the first input of the OR-gate
g_{w^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}}
is an AND-gate of

g^{\mathrm{i}}_{w^{\prime}}
and the output gate
g_{w^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime},1}
of a circuit coding
\varphi_{1}
on inputs including
the
g^{q_{P_{k}}}_{w^{\prime}_{k}}
and
g^{q_{N_{k}}}_{w^{\prime\prime}}
. We have
\nu(g^{\mathrm{i}}_{w^{\prime}})=1
because
\nu(w^{\prime})=1
,
and the second gate evaluates to
1
by construction of the circuit, as
witnessed by the Boolean valuation of the
g^{q_{P_{k}}}_{w^{\prime}_{k}}
and
g^{q_{N_{k}}}_{w^{\prime\prime}}
.
In the second case we follow the same reasoning but with the second input
to
g^{q^{\prime}}_{w^{\prime}}
instead, which is an AND-gate on

g^{\lnot\mathrm{i}}_{w^{\prime}}
and
\varphi_{0}
.

By induction on the run, the claim is proven, and applying it to the root of the
run concludes the proof of the first direction of the
equivalence
(for the
induction step of the induction on strata).

Backward direction.

We now prove the converse implication for the induction step of the induction on
strata, i.e., letting
i
be the current stratum, for every node
w
and state

q
with
\zeta(q)=i
, if
\nu(g^{q}_{w})=1
then there exists a run
\rho

of
A
starting at
w
. From the definition of the stratification
function
\zeta^{\prime}
of the cycluit from
\zeta
, we have
\zeta^{\prime}(g^{q}_{w})=\zeta(q)
, so as
\nu(g^{q}_{w})=1
we know that
\nu_{i}(g^{q}_{w})=1
, where

\nu_{i}
is the
i
-th
stratum evaluation
\nu_{i}
of
C
(remember Definition
29
).
By induction hypothesis on the strata, we know from the
equivalence
that, for any
j<i
, for any
gate
g^{q^{\prime\prime}}_{w^{\prime\prime}}
of
C
with
\zeta(g^{q^{\prime\prime}}_{w^{\prime\prime}})=j
, we have
\nu_{j}(g^{q^{\prime\prime}}_{w^{\prime\prime}})=1
iff
there exists a run
\rho
of
A
on
\nu(T)

starting at
w^{\prime\prime}

in state
q^{\prime\prime}
.

We show the claim by an induction on the iteration in the application of
Algorithm
1
for
\nu_{i}
where the gate
g^{q}_{w}
was
set to
1
. The base case concerns gates that were initially true before
applying the algorithm: by

Recall that the definition of
\nu_{i}
according to
Definition
29
proceeds in three steps. Initially, we
fix the value in
\nu_{i}
of gates of
lower strata, so we can then conclude by induction hypothesis on the strata. We
then set the value of all NOT-gates in
\nu_{i}
, but these cannot be of the form

g^{q^{\prime}}_{w^{\prime}}
so there is nothing to show. Last, we evaluate all other gates
with Algorithm
1
. We then show our claim by an
induction on the iteration in the application of
Algorithm
1
for
\nu_{i}
where the gate
g^{q}_{w}
was
set to
1
. The base case, where
g^{q}_{w}
was initially true, was covered in the
beginning of this paragraph.

For the induction step on the application of
Algorithm
1
,
when a gate
\nu_{i}(g^{q^{\prime}}_{w^{\prime}})
is set to true, as
\nu_{i}(g^{q^{\prime}}_{w^{\prime}})
is an
OR-gate by construction, from the workings of Algorithm
1
, there are two possibilities:
either its input AND-gate that includes
g^{\mathrm{i}}_{w^{\prime}}
was true,
or its input AND-gate that includes
g^{\neg\mathrm{i}}_{w^{\prime}}
was true. We prove the
first case, the second being analogous. From the fact that
g^{\mathrm{i}}_{w^{\prime}}
is
true, we know that
\nu(w^{\prime})=1
. Consider the other input gate to that AND
gate, which is the output gate of a circuit
C^{\prime}
reflecting

\varphi\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Delta(q^{\prime},(\lambda(w^{\prime}),\nu(w^{\prime})))
, with the input gates
adequately substituted. We consider the value by
\nu_{i}
of the gates that
are used as input gates of
C^{\prime}
in the construction of
C
(i.e., OR-gates, in
the case of variables that occur positively, or directly
g^{q^{\prime\prime}}_{w^{\prime}}
-gates,
in the case of variables that occur negatively). By construction of

C^{\prime}
, the corresponding Boolean valuation
\nu^{\prime}
is a witness to the satisfaction of
\varphi
. By induction hypothesis on
the strata (for the negated inputs to
C^{\prime}
; and for the non-negated inputs to

C^{\prime}
which are in a lower stratum) and on the step at which the gate was
set to true by Algorithm
1
(for the inputs in the same stratum, which must be positive), the
valuation of these inputs reflects the existence of the corresponding runs.
Hence, we can assemble these (i.e., a leaf node in the first two cases, a run in
the third case) to obtain a run starting at
w^{\prime}
for state
q^{\prime}

using the Boolean valuation
\nu^{\prime}
of the variables of
\varphi
; this valuation satisfies

\varphi
as we have argued.

This concludes the two inductions of the proof of the
equivalence
for the
induction step of the induction on strata, which concludes the proof.
∎

Putting it together.

We now conclude the proof of Theorem
24
by explaining how this
provenance construction for
\overline{\Gamma}
-SATWAs can be used to compute
the provenance of an ICG-Datalog query on a treelike instance. This is again
similar to
[
ABS15b
]
.

Recall the definition of tree encodings from
Appendix
.1
, and the
definition of the alphabet
\Gamma^{k}_{\sigma}
. To represent the dependency of
automaton runs on the presence of individual facts, we will be working with

\overline{\Gamma^{k}_{\sigma}}
-trees, where the Boolean annotation on a node
n
indicates
whether the fact coded by
n
(if any) is present or absent. The semantics
is that we map back the result to
\Gamma^{k}_{\sigma}
as follows:

Definition 50
.

We define the mapping
\varepsilon
from
\overline{\Gamma^{k}_{\sigma}}
to

\Gamma^{k}_{\sigma}
by:

•

\varepsilon((d,s),1)
is just
(d,s)
, indicating that the fact
of
s
(if any) is kept;

•

\varepsilon((d,s),0)
is
(d,\emptyset)
, indicating that the fact of

s
(if any) is removed.

We abuse notation and also see
\varepsilon
as a mapping from

\overline{\Gamma^{k}_{\sigma}}
-trees to
\Gamma^{k}_{\sigma}
-trees by applying it
to each node of the tree.

As our construction of provenance applies to automata on

\overline{\Gamma^{k}_{\sigma}}
, we show the following easy
lifting lemma

(generalizing Lemma 3.3.4 of
[
Ama16
]
):

Lemma 51
.

For any
\Gamma^{k}_{\sigma}
-SATWA
A
, we can compute in linear time a

\overline{\Gamma^{k}_{\sigma}}
-SATWA
A^{\prime}
such that, for any

\overline{\Gamma^{k}_{\sigma}}
-tree
E
, we have that
A^{\prime}
accepts
E
iff
A

accepts
\varepsilon(E)
.

Proof.

The proof is exactly analogous to that of Lemma 3.3.4
of
[
Ama16
]
.
∎

We are now ready to conclude the proof of our main provenance result
(Theorem
24
):

Proof of Theorem
24
.

Given the program
P
and instance
I
, use
Theorem
22
to compute in
FPT-linear time in
\left|P\right|
a
\Gamma^{k}_{\sigma}
-SATWA
A
that tests it on tree
encodings of width
\leqslant k_{\text{I}}
, for
k_{\text{I}}
the treewidth bound.
Compute also in FPT-linear time a tree encoding
E
of the instance
I
, i.e., a

\Gamma^{k}_{\sigma}
-tree
E
, using Lemma
41
.
Lift the
\Gamma^{k}_{\sigma}
-SATWA
A
in linear time using
Lemma
51
to a
\overline{\Gamma^{k}_{\sigma}}
-SATWA
A^{\prime}
, and use Theorem
32
on
A^{\prime}
and
E
to compute in
FPT-linear time a stratified cycluit
C^{\prime}
that captures the provenance of
A^{\prime}

on
E
: the inputs of
C^{\prime}
correspond to the nodes of
E
.
The treewidth of
C^{\prime}
is in
O(A^{\prime})
, so it is FPT-linear in
\left|P\right|
.
Let
C
be obtained
from
C^{\prime}
in linear time by changing the inputs of
C^{\prime}
as follows: those which
correspond to nodes
n
of
E
containing a fact (i.e., with label
(d,s)
for

\left|s\right|=1
) are renamed to be an input gate that stands for the fact of
I

coded in this node; the nodes
n
of
E
containing no fact are replaced by a
0-gate, i.e., an OR-gate with no inputs. Clearly,
C
is still a stratified
Boolean cycluit that satisfies the required treewidth bound,
and
C_{\mathsf{inp}}
is exactly the set of facts of
I
.

All that remains to show is that
C
captures the provenance of
P
on
I
in
the sense of Definition
23
. To see why, consider an arbitrary
Boolean valuation
\nu
mapping the facts of
I
to
\{0,1\}
, and call
\nu(I)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{F\in I\mid\nu(F)=1\}
. We must show that
\nu(I)
satisfies
P
iff

\nu(C)=1
. By construction of
C
, it is obvious that
\nu(C)=1
iff

\nu^{\prime}(C^{\prime})=1
, where
\nu^{\prime}
is the Boolean valuation of
C^{\prime}_{\mathsf{inp}}
defined by
\nu^{\prime}(n)=\nu(F)
when
n
codes some fact
F
in
E
, and
\nu^{\prime}(n)=0
otherwise. By
definition of the provenance of
A^{\prime}
on
E
, we have
\nu^{\prime}(C^{\prime})=1
iff
A^{\prime}

accepts
\nu^{\prime}(E)
, that is, by definition of lifting, iff
A
accepts

\varepsilon(\nu^{\prime}(E))
.
Now all that remains to observe is that
\varepsilon(\nu^{\prime}(E))
is precisely a tree
encoding of the instance
\nu(I)
: this is by definition of
\nu^{\prime}
from
\nu
,
and by definition of our tree encoding scheme (see “subinstance-compatibility”
in
[
Ama16
]
). Hence, by definition of
A
testing
P
, the tree

\varepsilon(\nu^{\prime}(E))
is accepted by
A
iff
\nu(I)
satisfies
P
. This finishes
the chain of equivalences, and concludes the proof of
Theorem
24
.
∎

.7
From cycluits to circuits

Theorem

(
34
)
.

There is an
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
s.t.,
for any stratified cycluit
C
of treewidth
k
, we can compute in time

O(2^{k^{\alpha}}\left|C\right|)
a circuit
C^{\prime}
which is equivalent to
C
and has
treewidth
O(2^{k^{\alpha}})
.

The proof of Theorem
34
is quite technical and proceeds in
several successive stages, corresponding to the following sections. In all
sections but the last one, we focus on
monotone cycluits
. Throughout the
whole proof, we assume without loss of generality that there are no

isolated gates
, i.e., gates that have no input gate and being the input to
no gate (because such gates are not necessarily reflected in tree decompositions). We call a
0-gate
an OR-gate with no inputs, and a
1-gate

an AND-gate with no inputs.

.7.1
Rewriting to arity-two cycluits

For technical convenience, it will be easier to work with
arity-two
cycluits
:

Definition 52
.

The
fan-in
of a gate
g
in a monotone cycluit
C
is the number of gates
g^{\prime}

such that
g^{\prime}\rightarrow g
is a wire of
C
(note that, as we are working with
cycluits, this may include
g^{\prime}=g
, i.e.,
g
may be its own input). For
c\in\mathbb{N}
, a monotone cycluit
C

is
arity-
c
if
each AND- and OR-gate has fan-in at most
c
, and if no gate is its own input
(i.e., there is no wire of the form
g\rightarrow g
).

It is obvious that monotone cycluits, just like circuits, can be rewritten in linear time
to an
arity-two
monotone cycluit, by taking advantage of the associativity of the OR and
AND Boolean operations: we just rewrite each AND- and OR-gate with fan-in
>2

to a tree of gates of fan-in
\leqslant 2
that computes the required Boolean
operation on the original set of inputs.

What is more technical to show, however, is that this rewriting operation can be
performed on a
treelike
cycluit, while ensuring that the treewidth of the
result is still only a function of the treewidth of the original cycluit.
Hence, the result that we wish to prove is the following:

Lemma 53
.

For any monotone cycluit
C
and tree decomposition
T
of width
k
of
C
,
we can compute in time
O(\left|C\right|+\left|T\right|)
a monotone arity-two cycluit

C^{\prime}
which is equivalent to
C
, and a tree decomposition
T^{\prime}
of width

k^{2}
of
C^{\prime}
.

Proof.

First, to ensure the condition that no gate of the circuit is its own input,
observe that any wire of the form
g\rightarrow g
can be dropped if
g
is an OR-gate
with other inputs; if
g
is an OR-gate whose only input is itself, then it
will never evaluate to
1
so we can replace it by a
0
-gate, and if
g

is an AND-gate with a wire
g\rightarrow g
then we can replace
g
by a
0
-gate as
it will never evaluate to
1
. This can be done in linear time and does not
increase the width of the tree decomposition.

We assume without loss of generality that the tree decomposition
T
has arity
at most two (i.e., each bag has at most two children), as we can otherwise
ensure it by rewriting
T
in linear time (by replacing each bag with more
than two children with a hierarchy of bags with the same contents and with two
children). We assign each wire
g\rightarrow g^{\prime}
of the circuit to some
bag
b
of
T
such that
g,g^{\prime}\in\mathrm{dom}(b)
: this is doable in linear time by
Lemma 3.1 of
[
FFG02
]
.
We now perform a bottom-up
traversal of the tree decomposition
T
.
In this process, we will annotate bags
b
of the tree decomposition with sets
of pairs of gates
g\mapsto g^{\prime}
, indicating that
g
is to be renamed to
g^{\prime}

in the subtree of
T
rooted at
b
.
During the bottom-up traversal, we memorize, for each gate
g
in the
current bag
b
, its fan-in for the wires that we have seen so far: it is
initially
0
, and is computed at bag
b
as the sum of the fan-in of
g
at
all the child bags (if any) plus its fan-in for the wires assigned to bag
b
.
Whenever the fan-in of a gate
g
at a bag
b
exceeds two, we do the following:
we create one gate
g_{1}
and label the first child bag of
b
(if it exists)
with
g\mapsto g_{1}
,
create one gate
g_{2}
and label the second child bag of
b
(if it exists)
with
g\mapsto g_{2}
, and create a gate
g^{\prime}
for the wires of the current bag. We let the
input gates of
g^{\prime}
be the input gates present at the current bag, and rewrite
this to arity-two, which introduces
\leqslant k-1
new gates in total.
Now, we set the input gates of
g
to be
g^{\prime\prime}
and
g_{1}
, where
g^{\prime\prime}
is a
fresh gate for the same operation as
g
, and set the input gates of
g^{\prime\prime}
to
be
g_{2}
and
g^{\prime}
. We have introduced
\leqslant k
new gates in the current bag
per gate initially in
b
, so the new size of
b
is at most
k^{2}
.

Once this pass is done, we perform a top-down pass to perform the
renamings indicated on the bags, which is doable in linear time because the number of renamings to
perform at any stage is constant (it is bounded by the width of the bags).

It is clear that this process produces an arity-two cycluit equivalent to
C
,
and the rewritten tree decomposition
T^{\prime}
has width
k^{2}
.
∎

.7.2
Regrouped tree decompositions

Now that we can rewrite monotone cycluits to arity-two monotone cycluits in
linear time with only a quadratic blowup in the treewidth, we show that we can
impose an additional condition on tree decompositions of arity-two circuits,
which we call being
regrouped
:

Definition 54
.

A tree decomposition
T
of a cycluit
C=(G,W,g_{0},\mu)
is
regrouped
if it satisfies
the following property: for any gate
g\in G
, letting
\mathrm{ins}(g)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{g^{\prime}\in G\mid g^{\prime}\rightarrow g\in W\}
, there is a bag
b_{g}
of
T
such that
\{g\}\cup\mathrm{ins}(g)\subseteq\mathrm{dom}(b)
.

In other words, a tree decomposition is regrouped if each gate has a witness bag
which contains that gate and
all
of its inputs. This can be contrasted
with the usual notion of tree decomposition, where each of the input wires can
be witnessed by a different bag. We show that, for arity-two cycluits, we can
assume that tree decompositions are regrouped in this sense:

Lemma 55
.

For any monotone arity-two cycluit
C
and tree decomposition
T
of
C
of
width
k
, we
can compute in time
O(\left|C\right|+\left|T\right|)
a regrouped tree decomposition

T^{\prime}
of
C
of width
\leqslant 3k
.

Proof.

We define
T^{\prime}
as having same skeleton as
T
and define each
b^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}
,
letting
b
be the corresponding bag in
T
, to have
\mathrm{dom}(b^{\prime})=\mathrm{dom}(b)\cup\bigcup\{\mathrm{ins}(g)\mid g\in b\}
. In other words, we add to each bag in
T^{\prime}
the
input gates of the gates that occur there. It is clear that
T^{\prime}
then
satisfies the regrouped condition: take as witnessing bag
b_{g}
of a gate

g
any bag
b^{\prime}
of
T^{\prime}
corresponding to a bag
b\in T
such that
g\in\mathrm{dom}(b)
. It is clear that
T^{\prime}
has width at most
3k
, as each bag
b^{\prime}

of
T^{\prime}
contains at most the contents of the corresponding bag
b
of
T
plus
two additional input gates for each gate occurring in
b
, thanks
to the fact that
C
is arity-two.

As the regrouped condition subsumes the condition of tree decompositions that
requires an occurrence of every wire, the only thing left to show is that the
occurrences of any gate
g
in
T^{\prime}
still forms a connected subtree. As
T
is
a tree decomposition of
C
, let
T_{g}
be the connected subtree containing the
occurrences of
g
in
T
, and, for every
g^{\prime}
such that
g\in\mathrm{ins}(g^{\prime})
, let
T_{g^{\prime}}
be the
connected subtree of the occurrences of
g^{\prime}
in
T
. For any
g^{\prime}
such that
g\in\mathrm{ins}(g^{\prime})
, as

T
is a tree decomposition, it must witness the wire
g\rightarrow g^{\prime}
, so the
subtrees
T_{g}
and
T_{g^{\prime}}
intersect in
T
. Now, the subtree
T^{\prime}_{g}
of the
occurrences of
g
in
T^{\prime}
is by construction the analogue in
T^{\prime}
of the
union of the connected subtrees
T_{g}
and
T_{g^{\prime}}
for
g^{\prime}
such that
g\in\mathrm{ins}(g^{\prime})
. We
conclude because the union of connected subtrees that intersect is also a
connected subtree.
∎

.7.3
Normal-form tree decompositions

Having presented the notion of regrouped tree decompositions, we now introduce
the notion of
normal-form
tree decompositions, which satisfy the
regrouped conditions and satisfy other additional conditions. Unlike what we
have done so far, for technical reasons that will become apparent in the next
section,
we will define these tree decompositions as
undirected

connected trees rather than
rooted
trees: this makes no difference, as we
used to consider tree decompositions as rooted trees simply for convenience. We
call
T
an
unrooted
tree decomposition if it is a tree decomposition
whose underlying tree is unrooted, and, for
b\in T
, the
rooting
of
T

at bag
b
is a tree decomposition, in the usual rooted sense, obtained by
picking
b
as the root in this tree; this always yields a valid tree
decomposition according to our earlier definitions.

Definition 56
.

An unrooted tree decomposition
T
of a monotone cycluit
C
is in
normal form
if
it satisfies the following additional conditions:

1.

Each bag
b
of
T
has (undirected) degree either
1
or
3
; we call

b
a
leaf bag
if it has degree
1
, and an
internal bag
if
it has degree
3
.

2.

There is an injective mapping
\varphi
from each gate
g
of
C
to a leaf bag

b_{g}
with
\mathrm{dom}(b_{g})=\{g\}\cup\mathrm{ins}(g)
, where
\mathrm{ins}(g)
are the input gates
of
g
. The

\varphi
-occurrences
of
g
are the leaf bags
\{\varphi(g)\}\sqcup\{\varphi(g^{\prime})\mid g^{\prime}\in\mathrm{ins}(g)\}
.

3.

The tree decomposition is
scrubbed
, meaning that:

•

if a gate
g
occurs in a leaf bag
b
, then
b
is a

\varphi
-occurrence of
g
;

•

if a gate
g

occurs in a non-leaf bag
b
then there are two
\varphi
-occurrences
b_{1}\neq b_{2}
of
g
and two neighbor bags
b_{1}^{\prime}\neq b_{2}^{\prime}

of
b
(note that we may have
b_{i}=b_{i}^{\prime}
),
such that, for
i\in\{1,2\}
, the unique simple path from
b
to
b_{i}
goes through

b_{i}^{\prime}
.

Observe that the first condition ensures that, for any leaf bag
b

of
T
, the rooting of
T
at bag
b
is a tree decomposition where the root
bag
b
has exactly one child, and all other bags have exactly two children
except the leaf bags which have zero. The second condition implies that
T
, or
indeed any rooting of
T
, is regrouped, with the witnesses for regrouping being chosen
at the leaf bags in an injective fashion. The third condition
intuitively ensures that each gate
g
occurs in bags of
T
precisely in its

\varphi
-occurrences and on the
paths between the
\varphi
-occurrences of
g
. In particular, the first point of
the third condition implies that any leaf bag with non-empty domain must be in
the image of
\varphi
. We also call a rooted tree decomposition
scrubbed

when it satisfies the analogous condition (with undirected paths).

We now show:

Lemma 57
.

For any monotone arity-two cycluit
C
and regrouped tree decomposition
T

of
C
of width
k
, we can compute in time
O(\left|C\right|+\left|T\right|)
a
normal-form tree decomposition
T^{\prime}
of
C
of width
k
.

Proof.

We modify
T
in linear time in the following way to satisfy the second
condition.
For each gate
g
, letting
b_{g}
be the witness bag for
g
guaranteed by
replace
b_{g}
by a new bag

b_{g}^{\prime}
with
\mathrm{dom}(b_{g}^{\prime})=\mathrm{dom}(b_{g})
, whose first child is
b_{g}
and whose
second child is a bag
b_{g}^{\prime\prime}
whose domain is
(\{g\}\cup\mathrm{ins}(g))
(note that this is a subset of
\mathrm{dom}(b_{g})
).
We can now set

\varphi(g)
to be
b_{g}^{\prime\prime}
, with
\varphi
being injective.

We then rewrite
T
in linear time in the usual way to ensure that each bag
has at most two children.

We next make
T
scrubbed in three linear time passes. First, we remove all gates
g
in leaf bags
b
when
b
is not a

\varphi
-occurrence of
g
; this removal does not affect the fact that
T
is a
tree decomposition, because each wire is still covered at the
\varphi
-image of
its target gate, and the occurrences of each gate still form a connected
subtree as we are only removing occurrences at leaf bags. Second, we do a
bottom-up pass to compute, for each occurrence of a gate
g
at a bag
b
, a
value
n_{g,b}
indicating how
many different children of
b
have a
\varphi
-occurrence of
g
as a descendent
(this number is between
0
and
2
): if this number is
0
at an internal
bag, then we remove
g
. The removal of
g
in this case does not violate the
fact that
T
is a tree decomposition: indeed, it does not affect the fact
that the wires are covered, because this is still the case at the

\varphi
-images, which are not internal bags; and gate occurrences still form
connected subtrees, because whenever we remove a gate
g
from a bag
b
then there is
only one neighbor of
b
at most (namely, its parent) where
g
also occurs.
Third, we do a top-down pass where,
whenever we have
n_{g,b}=1
for a gate
g
at a bag
b
when we encounter

b
top-down (i.e.,

n_{g,b}=1
and
n_{g,b^{\prime}}=1
for all ancestors of
b^{\prime}
where
g
occurs),
then we remove
g
from
b
.
The result is still a tree decomposition, because again wires are still covered at
the
\varphi
-images, and whenever we remove a gate
g
from a bag
b
then there is only one
neighbor of
b
at most (namely, one of its children) where
g
also occurs.

We now let
T^{\prime}
be the result of forgetting the orientation of
T
. It is
clear that the first point of the third condition is satisfied, and the second
point is satisfied for any occurrence of a gate
g
at an internal bag
b
:
indeed,
b
either had two different children having a

\varphi
-occurrence of
g
as a descendant, or
b
had one such descendant and had an
ancestor
b^{\prime}
whose parent
b^{\prime\prime}
had a
\varphi
-occurrence as a descendent of
its other child. Hence,
T^{\prime}
is scrubbed.

We last ensure that
T^{\prime}
satisfies the first condition. Note that, as each bag
of
T
had at most two children, the undirected degree of bags of
T
is at
most
3
, so we can simply enforce the condition by adding bags with empty
domain. This concludes the proof.
∎

.7.4
Rewriting monotone cycluits

Armed with normal-form tree decompositions and the lemmas required to obtain
them, we are now ready to show our rewriting result for
monotone

cycluits:

Theorem 58
.

There is an
\alpha\in\mathbb{N^{*}}
s.t.,
for any monotone cycluit
C
of treewidth
k
, we can compute in time

O(2^{k^{\alpha}}\cdot\left|C\right|)
a circuit
C^{\prime}
which is equivalent to
C
and has
treewidth
\leqslant 2^{k^{\alpha}}
.

To prove the theorem, we will rely on a notion of
partial assignment
of a
monotone cycluit, which we can use to enforce that some gates are evaluated
to
1
, even when they are not input gates:

Definition 59
.

For any monotone cycluit
C
and disjoint subsets
S^{+}\sqcup S^{-}
of gates that contain no input gate,
letting
S\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(S^{+},S^{-})
,
we define the
partial assignment

\rho_{S}(C)
to be the monotone cycluit obtained from
C
by replacing each
gate of
S^{+}
by a
1
-gate, and each gate of
S^{-}
by a
0
-gate.

We will also rely on a variant of Algorithm
1
to
evaluate parts of a cycluit in a tree decomposition based on the child parts in
the decomposition. It will work using the following notion:

Definition 60
.

The
constant gates
of a cycluit
C=(G,W,g_{0},\mu)
are the gates of
G\backslash C_{\mathsf{inp}}
that have no input gates,
i.e.,
C_{\mathsf{cst}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{g\in G\mid\nexists g^{\prime}\in G,g^{\prime}\rightarrow g\in W\}\backslash C_{\mathsf{inp}}
: in other words, these are precisely the
0
- and

1
-gates.
The
internal gates
of
G
are the gates which are
neither constant gates nor input gates.

An
evaluation partition
of a cycluit
C=(G,W,g_{0},\mu)

is a partition
G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}
of the internal gates of
C
.
The
frontier
of
G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}
is
F(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}F_{1}(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})\sqcup F_{2}(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})
, where
F_{j}(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{g\in G_{3-j}\mid\exists g^{\prime}\in G_{j},g\rightarrow g^{\prime}\in W\}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
.

This allows us to define Algorithm
3
, a variant
of Algorithm
1
which evaluates a cycluit by
evaluating separately the parts of its evaluation partition, memorizing only the
state of the frontier across evaluations. We claim the following:

Input:

Monotone cycluit
C=(G,W,g_{0},\mu)
,
Boolean valuation
\nu:C_{\mathsf{inp}}\to\{0,1\}
,
evaluation partition
G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}

Output:

\{g\in C\mid\nu(g)=1\}

\mathit{CST}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{g\in C_{\mathsf{inp}}\mid\nu(g)=1\}\cup\{g\in C_{\mathsf{cst}}\mid\mu(g)=\land\}

1

T_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathit{CST}

2

n\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left|F(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})\right|+2

3

for

i
from
1
to
n

do

4

for

j
from
1
to
2

do

5

U_{i,j}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}T_{i-1}\cap F_{j}(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})

6

V_{i,j}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\textup{SubEval}(C,\mathit{CST}\cup U_{i,j},G_{j})

7

T_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}V_{i,1}\cup V_{i,2}\cup\mathit{CST}

return

T_{n}

8

Function

\textup{SubEval}(C,Z,G^{\prime})

9

Input:

Monotone cycluit
C=(G,W,g_{0},\mu)
, subset
Z\subseteq G
of true
gates, subset
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
of iteration gates

Output:

Subset of gates that became true (observe the
similarity to Algorithm
1
)

Z_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}Z

10

l\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}0

11

do

12

l\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}l+1

13

Z_{l}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}Z_{l-1}\cup\Big\{g\in G^{\prime}\mid(\mu(g)={\lor}),\exists g^{\prime}\in Z_{l-1},g^{\prime}\rightarrow g\in W\Big\}

14

{}\qquad\qquad~\cup\Big\{g\in G^{\prime}\mid(\mu(g)={\land}),\{g^{\prime}\mid g^{\prime}\rightarrow g\in W\}\subseteq Z_{l}\Big\}

While

Z_{l}\neq Z_{l-1}

15

return

Z_{l}

16

Algorithm 3

Partition-based evaluation of monotone cycluits

Lemma 61
.

For any cycluit
C
, valuation
\nu
, and evaluation partition
G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}
of
C
, the result of Algorithm
3
is
indeed the set
g
of gates such that
g
evaluates to
1
in
C
under
\nu
.
In other words, the output is the same as that of
Algorithm
1
.

Proof.

Fix the cycluit
C
, valuation
\nu
, and evaluation partition
G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}
, let
F\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}F(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})
, and write
n\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left|F\right|+2
.

First, observe that the output on input gates and on constant gates is
correct, as all such gates that evaluate to
1
are added to
\mathit{CST}
and are
returned, and the ones that do not are never otherwise added to any
T_{i}

(remember that the
G_{j}
do not
contain any such gates, so we only add internal gates to the
Z_{l}
). Hence, it
suffices to restrict our attention to the internal gates.

The easy direction is the forward direction: let us show that, for any internal gate
g
in
T_{n}
, this
gate is also in the output of Algorithm
1
. We do so
by induction on the step of the execution at which the gate was added to one
of the sets
Z_{l}
. Initially, there are no internal gates in these sets.
For the induction, observe that the
gates added to such sets are either
\lor
-gates which have one input already
previously
in such a set (and so, by induction hypothesis, they are also in the output of
Algorithm
1
), or
\land
-gates where all inputs are
already in such a set (and we can use the induction hypothesis again), so
that indeed, when that same gate is considered in one iteration of
Algorithm
1
, it will also be added to the output of
that algorithm.

We now prove the backward direction. To do this, first observe that, for any

j\in\{1,2\}
, for any
1\leqslant i<n
, we have
U_{i,j}\subseteq U_{i+1,j}
. Indeed, we
have
U_{i,j}\subseteq V_{i,j}\subseteq T_{i}
, and all gates of

U_{i,j}
are gates of
F_{j}(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})
, so
U_{i+1,j}
contains all gates of
U_{i,j}
. Now, as
U_{i,j}

has size at most
\left|F(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})\right|
, this means that there is
i^{\prime}\leqslant\left|F(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})\right|+1
such that
U_{i^{\prime},j}=U_{i^{\prime}+1,j}
. As the
same argument applies to the other value of
j
, we deduce the existence of

1\leqslant i^{\prime}\leqslant\left|F(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})\right|+1
such that this holds for all
j\in\{1,2\}
. This clearly implies, then, that
T_{i^{\prime}}=T_{i^{\prime}+1}
, so that, as
we chose
n
to be sufficiently large,
we know that
T_{n}=T_{n-1}
.

We use this claim to conclude the proof of the backward direction.
Assume by way of contradiction that there is a gate
g
which is added to the
output of Algorithm
1
and which is not part of the
output of
Algorithm
3
, and choose
g
to be the first
gate that has this property (formally, as several such gates may be added in
the same iteration of Algorithm
1
, choose any gate
among the first ones). By our initial remark about the correctness on constant
gates and input gates,
g
must
be an internal gate. Let
\Gamma
be the subset of the inputs of
g
which
were evaluated to
1
at an iteration of
Algorithm
1
which is strictly before the iteration
where
g
was evaluated to
1
.
By definition of that algorithm,

\Gamma
is in the output of Algorithm
1
, and
hence, by minimality of
g
, it is in the output of
Algorithm
3
. Hence,
\Gamma\subseteq T_{n}
,
hence
\Gamma\subseteq T_{n-1}
by the claim that we showed.
Let us use this to study what happened during the
last iteration of Algorithm
3
and reach a
contradiction.

As
g
is an internal gate, let
j\in\{1,2\}
such that
g\in G_{j}
, and
let us show that
g\in V_{n,j}
, by considering the invocation

\textup{SubEval}(C,\mathit{CST}\cup U_{n,j},G_{j})
.
It suffices to show that there
is some
l\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\Gamma\subseteq Z_{l}
. Indeed, if this is the case,
then, taking a
minimal such
l
, we can consider the
(l+1)
-th iteration of the loop of

SubEval
: this iteration takes place, because
Z_{l}
has just changed
by addition of some gate in
\Gamma
(or, if

l=0
, it takes place by definition of a
do… while
loop), and in
this iteration we know that we have
g\in Z_{l+1}
, because
g
was set to
1
in
Algorithm
1
on the basis of
\Gamma
, and the
iterations in
SubEval
are defined in the same way on
G_{j}
. So let us show
that
\Gamma\subseteq Z_{l}
for some
l\in\mathbb{N}
. As the
Z_{l}
are monotone,
we can show this gate-per-gate: so let
g^{\prime}\in\Gamma
and show that it is
in
Z_{l}
for some
l\in\mathbb{N}
.

We do a case disjunction. First, if
g^{\prime}\in\mathit{CST}
, then
we have
g^{\prime}\in Z_{0}
, so there is nothing to show.
Second, assume that
g^{\prime}\in G_{j}
. We use the fact that

g^{\prime}\subseteq T_{n-1}
and
g^{\prime}\in G_{j}
to deduce that, by definition
of
T_{n-1}
,
we must have had
g^{\prime}\in\textup{SubEval}(C,\mathit{CST}\cup U_{n-1,j},G_{j})
. Now, as
U_{n-1,j}\subseteq U_{n,j}
, the monotonicity of

SubEval
ensures that we also have
g^{\prime}\in\textup{SubEval}(C,\mathit{CST}\cup U_{n,j},G_{j})
. Hence, there is indeed
l\in\mathbb{N}
during this
evaluation
such that
g^{\prime}\in Z_{l}
.
Third, assume that
g^{\prime}\in G_{3-j}
.
As
g^{\prime}
is an input gate of
g
, and
g\in G_{j}
, this implies that
g^{\prime}\in F_{j}(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})
. Hence, as

\Gamma\subseteq T_{n-1}
, we have
g^{\prime}\in U_{n,j}
, hence,
g^{\prime}\in Z_{0}
.

This shows that, indeed, in the last iteration (
i=n
) of
Algorithm
3
, for
j
defined such that
g\in G_{j}
, there is some iteration of the corresponding invocation of

SubEval
where all gates of
\Gamma
are added to
Z_{i}
, so that we must add
g
to

Z_{i+1}
. Hence,
g
is in the output of
Algorithm
3
, which contradicts our initial
assumption and concludes the proof.
∎

We are now ready to prove Theorem
58
:

Proof of Theorem
58
.

We will not define
\alpha
explicitly, but it will be apparent from the proof that a suitable

\alpha
can be defined.
Note that, up to adding a fresh gate to serve as the new distinguished output
gate, we can clearly ensure that the
distinguished output gate
g_{0}
has exactly one input, and that it is not an input gate to any other gate of
C
,
and we can modify
T
accordingly.
We start by computing in time
O(f(k)\cdot\left|C\right|)
, for some suitable
exponential
f
, a tree
decomposition
T
of
C

[
Bod96
]
.
We use Lemmas
53
,
55

and
57
to ensure that, up to increasing the degree of
the polynomial in the exponent of
f
,
we can assume that the monotone cycluit
C
is arity-two and that
the tree decomposition
T
is in normal form. We choose to root the
normal-form tree decomposition
T
at the bag
\varphi(g_{0})
where
g_{0}
is the
distinguished output gate. The
resulting
T
is such that
\varphi
maps each gate to a leaf bag, except the
distinguished output gate
g_{0}
which is mapped to the root; however, from our initial
modification of
C
and the fact that
T
is scrubbed, we know that
g_{0}
only
occurs at the root (specifically, the root bag contains
g_{0}
and its one
input gate). Hence, in the sequel, we will never apply
\varphi
to
g_{0}
, and so
we see
\varphi
as a mapping to the leaf bags of
T
. To simplify the induction, we
ensure that the root bag also has two children, by adding to it a child with
empty domain; this does not break any of the conditions on
T
.

We must now create the circuit
C^{\prime}
and its tree decomposition
T^{\prime}
. In fact,
we will create a
pre-tree-decomposition

T^{\prime}
of
C^{\prime}
, namely, a tree
with same skeleton as
T
, where each bag contains at most

2^{k^{\alpha}}
gates
of
C^{\prime}
, and which satisfies the following conditions: first,
all wires in
C^{\prime}
are
either between nodes of a same bag, or from nodes of a bag to nodes of a
parent bag (i.e., all wires go upward in
T^{\prime}
); second, each gate only occurs in
the bag where it is created, except the gates of
C^{\prime}_{\mathsf{inp}}=C_{\mathsf{inp}}
,
whose occurrences in
T^{\prime}
will correspond to a subset of the bags of
T
where
they occur. The
pre-tree-decomposition
T^{\prime}
can clearly be rewritten to a tree decomposition
in linear time and with a blowup of a factor
3
in the width: first, by
adding the input gates to the bags corresponding to all their actual occurrences
in
T
, and second by adding each
gate of
T^{\prime}
to its parent bag, which ensures that all wires are indeed covered.
Hence, we will only describe how to construct a pre-tree-decomposition
T^{\prime}

of
C^{\prime}
.

We will create
C^{\prime}
and
T^{\prime}
by a bottom-up
traversal of
T
. At each bag
b
of
T
, we will denote by
T_{b}
the subtree
of
T
rooted at
b
, we will write
\mathrm{dom}(T_{b})
to refer to all gates
occurring in
T_{b}
, and will write
C_{b}
to mean the sub-cycluit of
C

obtained by restricting to the gates of
\mathrm{dom}(T_{b})
and a subset of the wires
between them, namely, the wires whose target
g

is such that
\varphi(g)\in T_{b}
. In other words, when
g\in\mathrm{dom}(T_{b})
but

\varphi(g)
is not in
T_{b}
, then
g
occurs in
C_{b}
but it has no inputs, even though it may
be the case that both inputs also occur in
\mathrm{dom}(T_{b})
. Further, the
inputs of
C_{b}
are then precisely
C_{\mathsf{inp}}\cap\mathrm{dom}(T_{b})
.
Throughout the proof, for each bag
b
, we will partition
the gates of
\mathrm{dom}(b)
in two sets (clearly computable in linear time):

•

The
upward gates
, written
\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b)
, which are the gates

g
such that
\varphi(g)
is a descendant of
b
, as well as all the input gates

g
in
\mathrm{dom}(b)
and all constant gates
g
in
\mathrm{dom}(b)
.

•

The
downward gates
, written
\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b)
, which are all
other gates.

Intuitively, when processing the bag
b
, the
upward gates
are those which can
be evaluated from other gates of
T_{b}
(and from the valuation of the input
gates) based on what happens in
T_{b}
. (However, this evaluation may depend on
downward gates of
\mathrm{dom}(b)
, so that we may be unable to perform it fully.) By
contrast, the
downward gates
are those for which we have no complete information yet,
and whose evaluation will take place later

To rewrite each
C_{b}
to a circuit when processing
T
, what is relevant to us is
the behavior of
C_{b}
for each valuation of its inputs, in terms of how the
upward gates evaluate
depending on the value of the downward gates
.
Intuitively, to rewrite
C_{b}
, we need to describe the “function” that gives
us the value of the upward
gates (which is propagated to parent bags that we will rewrite later)
depending on each possible valuation of the downward gates (for which we do not know the value).
Specifically, for any subset
S^{+}\subseteq\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b)
,
letting

S\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(S^{+},\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b)\backslash S^{+})
, for any gate
\gamma\in\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b)
, and for any valuation
\nu
of the inputs of
C_{b}
, we wish
to know whether
\gamma
evaluates to
1
in
\nu(\rho_{S}(C_{b}))
. Recalling the
definition of partial assignments (Definition
59
), note that
this is well-defined because
S^{+}
contains no input gates. For brevity we write

\Pi_{b}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}2^{\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b)}\times\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b)

We are now ready to describe the inductive rewriting process. For each bag
b

of
T
, we will create a bag
b^{\prime}
of
T^{\prime}
containing gates of
C^{\prime}
. Remember
that the circuit
C^{\prime}
must have the same inputs as
C
; in particular we will
ensure that, for any
b\in T
, the cycluit
C_{b}
and the circuit
C^{\prime}_{b^{\prime}}

have the same inputs; further, by definition of the pre-tree-decomposition

T^{\prime}
, all wires will go upward in
T^{\prime}
, meaning that, for any
b^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}
, the
circuit
C^{\prime}_{b^{\prime}}
can always be fully evaluated from a valuation of its
inputs. From this observation, we will inductively guarantee that
each bag
b^{\prime}
in
T^{\prime}
, corresponding to bag
b
in
T
, will contain one gate
g_{b}^{S^{+},\gamma}
for each
(S^{+},\gamma)\in\Pi_{b}
, with the following semantics: for any valuation
\nu

of the inputs of
C_{b}
, the gate
g^{S^{+},\gamma}
evaluates to
1
in

\nu(C^{\prime}_{b^{\prime}})
iff
\gamma
evaluates to
1
in
\nu(\rho_{S}(C_{b}))
where
S\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(S^{+},\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b)\backslash S^{+})
.

We first describe the base case of the construction. Remember that, as
T
is scrubbed, each
leaf bag of
T
is either in the image of
\varphi
or has empty domain.
For a leaf bag
b
of
T
with empty domain,
there is nothing to do. Otherwise, let
g
be the preimage of
b
by
\varphi
,
i.e., the unique
g
(because
\varphi
is injective, and it is surjective on leaf
bags with non-empty domain) such that
b=\varphi(g)
. We then
have
\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b)=\{g\}\cup(C_{\mathsf{inp}}\cap\mathrm{dom}(b))
and
\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b)=\mathrm{ins}(g)\setminus C_{\mathsf{inp}}
; observe that, as
C
is
arity-two, it has no self-loops, so that
\mathrm{ins}(g)
and
\{g\}
are disjoint.
If
g
is an input gate, then
\mathrm{ins}(g)=\emptyset
and the
only gate to create in
b^{\prime}
is
g_{b}^{\emptyset,g}
which can simply be taken to
be
g
itself and satisfy the required conditions. If
g
is not an input
gate, the gates to define are
g_{b}^{S^{+},g}
for all
S^{+}\subseteq\mathrm{ins}(g)\backslash C_{\mathsf{inp}}
(which has
size at most 2), and we can simply realize this
following the truth table of the
operation
\mu(g)
of
g
, i.e., by setting
g_{b}^{S^{+},g}
to be a sub-circuit with
input
\mathrm{dom}(b)\cap C_{\mathsf{inp}}

whose value is the value of
g
in
\rho_{S}(C_{b})
with
S=(S^{+},\mathrm{ins}(g)\setminus(C_{\mathsf{inp}}\cup S^{+}))
.

We now describe the inductive case of the construction. We consider an internal bag
b
which has
two children
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
in
T
; we write
b^{\prime}
,
b_{1}^{\prime}
,
b_{2}^{\prime}
the
corresponding bags in
T^{\prime}
, with
b_{1}^{\prime}
and
b_{2}^{\prime}
being already inductively
defined.
In particular, we know that
b_{1}^{\prime}
and
b_{2}^{\prime}
contain gates (which for brevity we
will write
g_{1}^{S^{+},\gamma}
and
g_{2}^{S^{+},\gamma}
, rather than

g_{b^{\prime}_{1}}^{S^{+},\gamma}
and
g_{b^{\prime}_{2}}^{S^{+},\gamma}
) with the prescribed
properties; and we will omit for brevity the
b
subscript when defining gates
of
b^{\prime}
in
C^{\prime}
standing for the current bag
b
of
T
. For
i
from
0

to
(k+1)+2
,
we will now define gates
g^{S^{+},\gamma,i}
in
b^{\prime}
as follows, for all

(S^{+},\gamma)\in\Pi_{b}
:

•

Case of constant gates: if
\gamma
is a
1
-gate
(resp., a
0
-gate),
then the gate
g^{S^{+},\gamma,i}
is a
1
-gate (resp., a

0
-gate) for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant k+3
.

•

Case of input gates: if
\gamma
is an input gate, then the
gate
g^{S^{+},\gamma,i}
is the same input gate for all
0\leqslant i\leqslant k+3
.

•

Base case:
the gate
g^{S^{+},\gamma,0}
is simply a
0
-gate.

•

Induction:
letting
j
be such that
\varphi(\gamma)
is a descendant of
b_{j}
(so that

\gamma\in\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b_{j})
and
\gamma\in\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b_{3-j})
if
\gamma\in\mathrm{dom}(b_{3-j})
),
the gate
g^{S^{+},\gamma,i}
for
i\geqslant 1
is
an OR-gate, for all
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b_{j})\cap\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b_{3-j})

(which is necessarily
\subseteq\mathrm{dom}(b)
),
of the AND of
g^{S^{+},\gamma^{\prime},i-1}
for all
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}

and of
g_{j}^{(S^{+}\cap\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b_{j}))\cup\Gamma^{\prime},\gamma}
.

We simply let
g^{S^{+},\gamma}
to be
g^{S^{+},\gamma,k+3}
. It is clear that we
create only exponentially many gates at each bag of
T^{\prime}
, so that we can obey
the prescribed treewidth bound (in addition to the transformations of
C
that
we have performed at the beginning, namely, transformation to arity-2, and
up to the use of a normal-form tree decomposition which implies that the bag
size may be multiplied by
3
).

We now show the correctness of this construction at
b
, i.e., we check
inductively whether the gates that we create satisfy their prescribed
semantics.
Fixing
(S^{+},\gamma)\in\Pi_{b}
and a valuation
\nu
of the inputs of
C_{b}
, we consider the monotone
cycluit
\rho_{S}(C_{b})
and its evaluation under
\nu
, where
S\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(S^{+},\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b)\backslash S^{+})

(note that this does not include any input gates because
\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b)
does not).
What we must show is that
g^{S^{+},\gamma}

evaluates to
1
under
\nu
in
C^{\prime}
iff

\gamma
evaluates to
1
in
\nu(\rho_{S}(C_{b}))
.
To show this claim, we must connect our construction of the
g^{S^{+},\gamma,i}

to the evaluation of the cycluit
\nu(\rho_{S}(C_{b}))
; we will do so using the
notion of an evaluation partition, by defining one from the structure of
T_{b}
.

Specifically, consider the evaluation partition
G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}

of the internal gates of
\rho_{S}(C_{b})
defined by putting in
G_{1}
the internal gates
whose image by
\varphi
is a descendant of
b_{1}
, and in
G_{2}
those whose
image is a descendant of
b_{2}
. We show the following claims:

•

This partition indeed covers the
internal gates of
\rho_{S}(C_{b})
.
Indeed, all internal gates of
C_{b}
are
either gates of
\mathrm{dom}(T_{1})\backslash\mathrm{dom}(b)
(in which case their

\varphi
-image must be in
T_{1}
), or gates of
\mathrm{dom}(T_{2})\backslash\mathrm{dom}(b)

(same reasoning), or gates of
\mathrm{dom}(b)
in which case they must be in

\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b)
(as the gates of
\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b)
are constant in
\rho_{S}(C_{b})
) and then
their
\varphi
-image is a descendant of
b
by definition. Now, the
\varphi
-image cannot be
b

itself as
\varphi
maps only to leaves, so it must be either a descendant
of
b_{1}
or of
b_{2}
.

•

For each
j\in\{1,2\}
, we have
F_{j}(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})=\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b_{3-j})\cap\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b_{j})
.
Indeed, for the forward inclusion, any gate
g
of
F_{j}(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})
is a gate of
G_{3-j}
, that is,
\varphi(g)
is a descendant
of
b_{3-j}
. Further, there is a
wire
(g,g^{\prime})
with
g^{\prime}\in G_{j}
, that is,
\varphi(g^{\prime})
is a descendant
of
b_{j}
, and
g
occurs in
\varphi(g^{\prime})
. Now, as
g\in\mathrm{dom}(T_{j})\cap\mathrm{dom}(T_{3-j})
, as
T
is a tree decomposition, we have
g\in\mathrm{dom}(b_{j})

and
g\in\mathrm{dom}(b_{3-j})
, and more specifically we have
g\in\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b_{3-j})
and
g\in\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b_{j})
by what precedes. Conversely, any gate
g
of

\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b_{3-j})
is such that
\varphi(g)
is a descendant of
b_{3-j}
, so
that
g\in G_{3-j}
. Further, as
g\in\mathrm{dom}(b_{j})
, as the tree
decomposition
T
is scrubbed, there is a descendant bag
b
of
b_{j}

where
g
appears in a
\varphi
-image, and as
\varphi(g)
is not a descendant
of
b_{j}
it must be the case that
g
appears in the
\varphi
-image of
some
g^{\prime}
(so that
g^{\prime}\in G_{j}
) such that
g\rightarrow g^{\prime}
is a wire. Hence,
g\in G_{3-j}
and
g
is an input of a gate in
G_{j}
, so indeed
g\in F_{j}(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})
.

•

We have
\left|F(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})\right|\leqslant k+1
.
This follows from the
previous point. Indeed, we have
\left|F(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})\right|=\left|F_{1}(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})\right|+\left|F_{2}(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})\right|\leqslant\left|\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b_{1})\right|+\left|\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b_{1})\right|\leqslant\left|\mathrm{dom}(b)\right|\leqslant k+1
because
T
is a tree decomposition
of width
\leqslant k
.

Now, consider the evaluation of

\rho_{S}(C_{b})
under
\nu
using the evaluation partition
G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}
,
implemented with Algorithm
3
: write

T_{0}(S),T_{1}(S),\ldots,T_{k+3}(S)
the sequence of sets defined in the
execution of the algorithm. (As this sequence may be shorter, if necessary we
pad it until
T_{k+3}(S)
by copying the last value.)
Nested in our induction over
T
, we now show by induction on
0\leqslant i\leqslant k+1

that, in
b^{\prime}
as we have
defined it,
for any
S^{+}\subseteq\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b)

and
\gamma\in\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b)
,
for any valuation
\nu
of the
inputs of
C_{b}
, the gate
g^{S^{+},\gamma,i}
evaluates to
1
under
\nu
iff

\gamma\in T_{i}(S)\cap\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b)
. For the base case of

i=0
, indeed
g^{S^{+},\gamma,0}
evaluates to true iff

\gamma\in T_{0}(S)\cap\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b)
by definition of
T_{0}(S)
. For the induction step, observe
that, by definition,
g^{S^{+},\gamma,i}
evaluates to true
iff, letting
j
be such that
\varphi(\gamma)
is a descendant of
b_{j}
,
there is some
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b_{j})\cap\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b_{3-j})

(equivalently, by the second bullet point above,
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq F_{j}(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})
) such
that
g^{S^{+},\gamma^{\prime},i-1}

evaluates to
1
for all
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}
and

g_{j}^{(S^{+}\cap\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b_{j}))\cup\Gamma^{\prime},\gamma}
evaluates to
1
.

For the forward direction of this induction claim (for the nested induction),
assume the existence of such a
\Gamma^{\prime}
, and show that
\gamma\in T_{i}(S)
(this suffices as we have
\gamma\in\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b)
by definition).
By the induction
hypothesis for the inner induction, as
g^{S^{+},\gamma^{\prime},i-1}

evaluates to
1
for all
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}
, we know that
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq T_{i-1}(S)
. By the induction hypothesis for the outer induction, as

g_{j}^{(S^{+}\cap\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b_{j}))\cup\Gamma^{\prime},\gamma}
evaluates to
1
, we know that

\gamma
evaluates to
1
in

\nu_{j}(\rho_{S_{j}}(C_{j}))
, where
S_{j}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}((S^{+}\cap\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b_{j}))\cup\Gamma^{\prime},\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b_{j})\backslash(S^{+}\cup\Gamma^{\prime}))
and
\nu_{j}
is the restriction of
\nu
to the inputs
of
C_{j}
. Now,
using Lemma
61
,
consider the execution of
Algorithm
3
(on
\rho_{S}(C_{b})
under
\nu
), and consider the invocation of

SubEval
for this
i
and
j
. We know that

\mathit{CST}
contains all input gates of
\rho_{S_{j}}(C_{j})
set to
1
and all
gates of
S^{+}
(these are constant gates of
\rho_{S}(C_{b})
), so they are
in
Z_{0}
; and as

\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq F_{j}(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})
and
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq T_{i-1}(S)
, we
know that
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq U_{i,j}
, so
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq Z_{0}
. Now,
as we know that
\gamma
evaluates to
1
under
\nu_{j}
in
\rho_{S_{j}}(C_{j})
,
consider
the evaluation of Algorithm
1
to witness this,
and observe that, by what precedes,
the invocation of
SubEval
that we are considering is such that

Z_{0}
contains all gates which are initially true in

\nu_{j}(\rho_{S_{j}}(C_{j}))
, namely, the input gates that evaluate to
1
, the
constant
1
-gates, and the gates of the first component of
S_{j}
,
specifically,
those of
\Gamma^{\prime}
and a subset of those of
S^{+}
. Hence, each time
Algorithm
1
sets a gate to
1
,
SubEval

also does. Hence,
\gamma
is also returned by
SubEval
, so indeed

\gamma\in T_{i}(S)
, which concludes the forward direction.

For the backward direction of the nested induction claim, assume that
\gamma\in T_{i}(S)\cap\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b)
, and show the existence of a suitable
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Let
j\in\{1,2\}
be such that
\gamma\in\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b_{j})
.
We
choose
\Gamma^{\prime}
to be
T_{i-1}(S)\cap(\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b_{j})\cap\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b_{3-j}))
, or,
in other words,
U_{i,j}
in Algorithm
3
.
Now, for any
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}
,
observe that
\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b)
(as
\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b_{3-j})
, we know
that
\varphi(\gamma^{\prime})
is a descendant of
b_{3-j}
, hence of
b
), so that

\gamma^{\prime}\in T_{i-1}(S)\cap\mathrm{dom}^{\uparrow}(b)
, and by induction hypothesis for the
inner induction we know that
g^{S^{+},\gamma^{\prime},i-1}
evaluates to
1
. Now we
will use the induction hypothesis for the outer induction to argue that

g_{j}^{(S^{+}\cap\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b_{j}))\cup\Gamma^{\prime},\gamma}
evaluates to
1
, by
showing that
\gamma
evaluates to true under
\nu_{j}
in
\rho_{S_{j}}(C_{j})
,
with the same notations for
\nu_{j}
and
S_{j}
as for the forward direction above.
To do this, as in the
forward direction, we consider the evaluation of
SubEval
at
iteration
i
and for the current
j
. In this evaluation, the gates that
are initially true are the constant
1
-gates (in particular those of
S^{+}
),
the input gates that are true under
\nu
, and the gates of
T_{i-1}(S)\cap F_{j}(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})
, that is, of
\Gamma^{\prime}
. In
\rho_{S_{j}}(C_{j})
, all gates
of
\Gamma^{\prime}
are also true, and the other gates are also true whenever they
appear in
C_{j}
: in other words, all gates that are in
Z_{0}
at the beginning
of this evaluation of
SubEval
are also initially true in

\nu_{j}(\rho_{S_{j}}(C_{j}))
except if they are gates that do not occur
in
\rho_{S_{j}}(C_{j})
(and have no inputs or outputs in
\rho_{S}(C_{b})
).
This property ensures that all gates that evaluate to true in

SubEval
, which are gates of
G_{j}
, can also evaluate to true in
Algorithm
1
thanks to
Lemma
61
.
Hence, indeed
\gamma
evaluates to true under
\nu_{j}
in
\rho_{S_{j}}(C_{j})
, so
we conclude that indeed
g_{j}^{(S^{+}\cap\mathrm{dom}^{\downarrow}(b_{j}))\cup\Gamma^{\prime},\gamma}

evaluates to
1
. This finishes the proof of the inner induction, and hence
concludes the correctness proof of the induction step of the outer induction.

To conclude the proof of the Theorem, it suffices to observe that the root bag

b^{\prime}_{\mathrm{r}}
of the rewriting
C^{\prime}
contains a gate
g\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}g_{\mathrm{r}}^{\emptyset,\{g_{0}\}}

where
g_{0}
is the output gate of
C
, whose semantics (by the outer
induction claim) is guaranteed to be the following: for any valuation
\nu
of

C
(hence, of
C^{\prime}
), the gate
g
evaluates to true under
\nu
in
C^{\prime}
iff

g_{0}
evaluates to true in
\nu(C)
. Hence, we set
g
as the output gate
of
C^{\prime}
, and we have shown that
C^{\prime}
is indeed equivalent to
C
. This concludes
the proof.
∎

.7.5
Isotropic rewritings of monotone cycluits

To prove Theorem
34
for non-monotone cycluits, we will of course
perform an induction on the strata. The challenge when doing so is that, when
rewriting a stratum, we may need to access the value of
all
gates of the lower
strata, not just a single output gate. So, in the induction step on strata, it
will not be sufficient to rewrite a stratum to a circuit with a single output
gate: we need to rewrite in a way where all gates of the circuit can be reused
by lower strata. We define this formally:

Definition 62
.

A circuit (or cycluit)
C^{\prime}

emulates
a cycluit
C
iff
C
and
C^{\prime}

have the same input gates, all gates of
C
are gates of
C^{\prime}
, and for any
valuation
\nu
of the inputs, for any gate
g
of
C
,
g
evaluates to true
in
C
under
\nu
iff it does in
C^{\prime}
under
\nu
.

Similarly to how we modified the circuit at the beginning of the proof of
Theorem
58
, it will be convenient to rewrite the circuit in
linear time to ensure that each gate of interest has exactly one input and is
not itself the input to another gate. We call such gates the
potential
outputs
:

Definition 63
.

A
potential output
of a circuit (or cycluit)
C
is a gate
g
of
C

which has exactly one input gate and is not itself the input to any gate.

We can very simply rewrite our input cycluit so that all gates of interest are
potential outputs:

Lemma 64
.

For any cycluit
C
of treewidth
k
, we can compute in linear time a cycluit

C^{\prime}
of treewidth
\leqslant 2k
that emulates
C
, such that, for each gate
g
of

C\setminus C_{\mathsf{inp}}
, the gate
g
in
C^{\prime}
is a potential output.

Proof.

Simply build
C^{\prime}
by renaming all gates of
C
that are not input gates to a
different name. Now, for each gate
g
of
C
, add gate
g
to
C^{\prime}
by setting
it to be an AND-gate whose one input is the gate
g^{\prime}
which is the renaming of
g
in
C^{\prime}
. This
clearly ensures that
g
evaluates to TRUE iff its renaming does, so that
C^{\prime}

indeed emulates
C
. Further, the process is clearly in linear time and the
treewidth bound is respected because we are simply duplicating each gate.
∎

This allows us to assume that we are working with a cycluit where all
“relevant” gates are not the input gates to other gates.
Thanks to this,
the process that we really need for an induction on strata is the following variant of
Theorem
58
:

Theorem 65
.

There is an
\alpha\in\mathbb{N^{*}}
s.t.,
for any monotone cycluit
C
of treewidth
k
, we can compute in time

O(2^{k^{\alpha}}\cdot\left|C\right|)
a circuit
C^{\prime}
that emulates
C
and has
treewidth
\leqslant 2^{k^{\alpha}}
.

Proof.

.
First note that, thanks to Lemma
64
,
we can process the input monotone cycluit
C
in linear time, keeping the
parametrization in the treewidth, such that all gates of the original input
cycluit are now potential output gates. Hence, it now suffices to construct a
cycluit
C^{\prime}
that
quasi-emulates
the cycluit
C
that we are working
with, i.e., only emulates the potential output gates of
C
: indeed,

C^{\prime}
will then emulate all gates of the original cycluit.
As in Theorem
58
, we will assume that
C
is arity-two, and that
it has a normal-form tree decomposition
T
.
Further, as in the proof of Theorem
58
, we will
construct a pre-tree decomposition
T^{\prime}
of the result
C^{\prime}
, i.e., all wires are either within one bag or between a gate of
a bag and a gate of a neighboring bag:
T^{\prime}
will have same skeleton as
T
when
forgetting about edge orientation.

We first notice that we can obtain the desired circuit
C^{\prime}
in a naive way,
which runs in quadratic time and does not satisfy the treewidth bound. To do so,
we can simply apply Theorem
58
, for each potential output

g
of
C
, on the tree decomposition
T^{b}
obtained by rooting
T
at
b\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\varphi(g)
. We could then build
the rewriting of
C
as a circuit
C^{\prime}
that quasi-emulates
C
, by taking the union of
the rewritings thus produced: these rewritings are disjoint except for the input
gates. Further, we can construct a pre-tree
decomposition
T^{\prime}
of
C^{\prime}
by taking all the pre-tree
decompositions produced in the applications of Theorem
58
,
